. By the logic used in the proof of Theorem
4.4
, we see that this is also a lower bound on the sensitivity, so

\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}=(U-L).

(1) Let
u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}
be two datasets with integer values of type
T
. There is at most one index
i^{*}
such that, without loss of generality,
u_{i}=u^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i<i^{*}
, and
u_{j}=u^{\prime}_{j+1}
for all
j>i^{*}
.

Consider the case where there is no such
i^{*}
. Then
u=u^{\prime}
, so
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)=\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u^{\prime})
and
|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)=\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u^{\prime})|=0
.

Now, consider the case where there is such an
i^{*}
.
We note that, for all
y\in T
, we have
y\boxplus 0=y
. Therefore,inserting a value
u_{i^{*}}=0
will not affect the final sums. It will also provide the effect that, for all
i\neq i^{*}
,
u_{i}=u^{\prime}_{i}
.

We can now borrow the logic from
Eq.

6
to see that

|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[u_{1},\ldots,u_{i}]-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[u^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{i}]|\leq|u_{i^{*}}-u^{\prime}_{i}|\leq\max\{|L|,U\}.

(7)

By
Corollary

6.18
and by induction, the sums will get no further apart from each other than they were in the expression above. Therefore,
|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u^{\prime})|\leq\max\{|L|,U\}
. By the logic used in the proof of Theorem
4.4
, we see that this is also a lower bound on the sensitivity, so

\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}=\max\{|L|,U\}.

∎

6.4.2
Randomized Permutation is a Transformation between Distance Metrics

Having shown that useful sensitivities can be established for ordered distance metrics, we show that randomized permutation provides a transformation from unordered distance metrics to ordered distance metrics. Using randomized permutation in combination with the theorems in Section
6.4.1
will then allow us to establish useful sensitivities for unordered distance metrics.

Theorem 6.19

(Coupling, Unknown
n
)
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{D}^{m}
and
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
. Additionally, let
\Pi:\mathcal{D}^{m}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{m},\Pi^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{n}
be randomized permutations of datasets
u
and
u^{\prime}
, respectively. For all datasets
u,u^{\prime}
such that
u\simeq_{\mathit{Sym}}u^{\prime}
, there exists a coupling
(\tilde{s}_{u},\tilde{s}_{u^{\prime}})
of the datasets
s_{u}=\Pi(x)
and
s_{u^{\prime}}=\Pi^{\prime}(u^{\prime})
such that
\tilde{s}_{u}\simeq_{ID}\tilde{s}_{u^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let
\mathrm{len}(u^{\prime})=\mathrm{len}(u)+1
. (In the
u\simeq_{\mathit{Sym}}u^{\prime}
case, we could have
\mathrm{len}(u)=\mathrm{len}(u^{\prime})
, but then we would have a trivial coupling where each ordering
\Pi(u)
is matched with the same ordering of elements
\Pi^{\prime}(u^{\prime})
, so we only consider the
|\mathrm{len}(u)=\mathrm{len}(u^{\prime})+1
case.) We can then think of
u^{\prime}
as being
u
, but with one “insertion”.

We now describe a way to enumerate
u
and
u^{\prime}
so that equivalent terms are matched. Let
\mathcal{J}[i]
be the
i^{\mathit{th}}
term in a sequence
\mathcal{J}
. Because
d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,u^{\prime})=1
, we know that
u
and
u^{\prime}
are identical from an unordered perspective, except
u^{\prime}
has one additional row. This means, then, that there is some sequence
\mathcal{J}
of terms
\{1,\ldots,m+1\}
such that, for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, we have
u_{i}=u^{\prime}_{\mathcal{J}[i]}
. As stated, there will be an unmatched
u^{\prime}
term since
n|=m+1
; we call this term
u^{\prime}_{\mathcal{J}[m+1]}
.

We now use this enumeration scheme to show that, for every permutation
\Pi(u)
, there is a permutation
\Pi^{\prime}(u^{\prime})
such that
d_{\mathit{ID}}(\Pi(u),\Pi^{\prime}(u^{\prime}))=1
. We first construct an intermediate ordering
u^{\prime\prime}
. Let
\Pi(u)_{k}
denote the
k^{\mathit{th}}
value in the vector
\Pi(u)
. Suppose that
\Pi(u)_{k}=u_{i}
. We pair this with
u_{\mathit{J}[i]}
. We proceed in this way to construct a dataset
u^{\prime\prime}
where
d_{\mathit{ID}}(\Pi(u),u^{\prime\prime})=0
.

Note that
u^{\prime\prime}
does not include the leftover term
u^{\prime}_{\mathcal{J}[m+1]}
. This means, then, that there are
(m+1)
permutations
\Pi^{\prime}(u^{\prime})
such that
d_{\mathit{ID}}(\Pi(u),\Pi^{\prime}(u^{\prime}))=1
. This construction applies to every
\Pi(u)
. Therefore, every
\Pi(u)
has
m+1
permutations
\Pi^{\prime}(u^{\prime})
such that
d_{\mathit{ID}}(\Pi(u),\Pi^{\prime}(u^{\prime}))=1
.

Recall that, without loss of generality, we are assuming that
m\geq n
. This means that
u
has
m!
permutations, while
u^{\prime}
has
n!
permutations. Note that our method pairs each permutation of
u
with
(|u|+1)
permutations of
u^{\prime}
, and the mapping from permutations of
u
to permutations of
u^{\prime}
is injective, with its inverse surjective. Because RP samples over permutations uniformly at random, we have shown that there is a coupling of randomness
(\tilde{s}_{u},\tilde{s}_{u^{\prime}})
between
s_{u}=\Pi(u)
and
s_{u^{\prime}}=\Pi(u^{\prime})
such that
\tilde{s}_{u}\simeq_{ID}\tilde{s}_{u^{\prime}}
.

∎

Corollary 6.20

(Coupling, Known
n
)
.

Let
\Pi:\mathcal{D}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{n}
be a randomized permutation of datasets of length
n
. For all datasets
u,u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
such that
u\simeq_{\mathit{CO}}u^{\prime}
, there exists a coupling
(\tilde{s}_{u},\tilde{s}_{u^{\prime}})
of the datasets
s_{u}=\Pi(u)
and
s_{u^{\prime}}=\Pi(u^{\prime})
such that
\tilde{s}_{x}\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}\tilde{s}_{u^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
u\simeq_{\mathit{CO}}u^{\prime}
. By Theorem
2.10
, this means that
d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,u^{\prime})=2
. Using the path property of
d_{\mathit{Sym}}
(Lemma
2.14
) and by Theorem
6.19
, then, there is a coupling of randomness such that
d_{\mathit{ID}}(\Pi(u),\Pi(u^{\prime}))\leq 2

[
46
]
. By Theorem
2.10
, then,
d_{\mathit{Ham}}(\Pi(u),\Pi(u^{\prime}))\leq 1
, so there is a coupling of randomness
(\tilde{s}_{x},\tilde{s}_{u^{\prime}})
between
s_{u}=\Pi(u)
and
s_{u^{\prime}}=\Pi(u^{\prime})
such that
\tilde{s}_{u}\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}\tilde{s}_{u^{\prime}}
.

∎

We remark that the proofs of Theorem
6.19
and Corollary
6.20
are
constructive
, and so not only do we show that the coupling
exists
, but we also show how to find it efficiently.

6.4.3
Sensitivities for Unordered Distance

Now that we have proven the coupling between unordered distance metrics and ordered distance metrics, we can finally prove the sensitivity of bounded sum with RP-based summation; i.e., Theorem
6.15
.

Proof of Theorem
6.15
.

We now prove each part of Theorem
6.15
, which shows that the implemented sensitivities for Method
6.14
match the idealized sensitivities of bounded sum, with respect to unordered distances between datasets.

1.

Method
6.14
first applies RP to the input dataset, and Theorem
6.19
tells us that RP transforms datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{Sym}}
to a coupling of datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{ID}}
. We can now work with datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{ID}}
. Theorem
6.16
tells us that
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}\leq\max\{|L|,U\}
, so we must also have
\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}\leq\max\{|L|,U\}
. By the logic used in the proof of Theorem
4.4
, we see that this is also a lower bound on the sensitivity, so

\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}=\max\{|L|,U\},

completing the proof.

2.

Method
6.14
first applies RP to the input dataset, and Corollary
6.20
tells us that RP transforms datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{CO}}
to a coupling of datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{Ham}}
. We can now work with datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{Ham}}
. Theorem
6.16
tells us that
\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}\leq(U-L)
, so we must also have
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}\leq(U-L)
. By the logic used in the proof of Theorem
4.4
, we see that this is also a lower bound on the sensitivity, so

\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}=U-L,

completing the proof.

∎

6.5
Sensitivity from Accuracy

In this section, we show a relationship between an upper bound on a function’s implemented sensitivity, and the function’s idealized sensitivity and accuracy. The sensitivities that we derive apply directly to an unaltered implementation of iterative summation. The accuracy of a summation function is dependent on the number of terms being summed (meaning the sensitivities we derive are dependent on the number of terms being summed), so we also introduce a method called
truncated summation
that allows these sensitivities to apply to the unbounded DP setting in which the number of terms being summed is not necessarily known to the data analyst.

Because these upper bounds on implemented sensitivity depend on a function’s accuracy, we also present methods from the numerical analysis literature for improving the accuracy of a summation function, and we compute the resulting sensitivities for these methods.

Lemma 6.21

(Relating Sensitivity and Accuracy)
.

\displaystyle\Delta\mathit{BS}^{*}\leq

\displaystyle\max_{u\simeq u^{\prime}}\left(|\mathit{BS}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u^{\prime})|+|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u)|+|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u^{\prime})-\mathit{BS}(u^{\prime})|\right).

Proof.

This follows immediately from two applications of the triangle inequality.
∎

The term
|\mathit{BS}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u^{\prime})|
is bounded by the idealized sensitivity. The terms
|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u)|
and
|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u^{\prime})-\mathit{BS}(u^{\prime})|
can be bounded by the accuracy of the function
\mathit{BS}^{*}
as compared to its idealized counterpart
\mathit{BS}
. We follow Higham, Wilkinson’s, and Kahan’s work on the accuracy of the sum function
[
24
,
49
,
31
]
for derivations of the accuracies of various summation strategies, including iterative summation.

Theorem 6.22

(Accuracy of Summation Algorithms
[
24
,
49
,
31
]
)
.

Let
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}:T^{n}_{[L,U]}\rightarrow T
be an implementation of the bounded sum function on the normal
(k,\ell)
-bit floats of type
T
, and let
\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}:\mathbb{R}^{n}_{[L,U]}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
compute the idealized bounded sum.

1.

If
\mathit{BS}^{*}
corresponds to iterative summation (Definition
5.1
), then for all
u\in T^{n}_{[L,U]}

\left|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(u)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)\right|\leq\frac{n^{2}}{2^{k+1}}\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

2.

If
\mathit{BS}^{*}
corresponds to pairwise summation (Definition
5.2
) and
n<2^{k}
, then for all
u\in T^{n}_{[L,U]}

\left|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(u)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)\right|\leq O\left(\dfrac{n\log n}{2^{k}}\right)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

3.

If
\mathit{BS}^{*}
corresponds to Kahan summation (Definition
5.3
) and
n<2^{k}
, then for all
u\in T^{n}_{[L,U]}

\left|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(u)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)\right|\leq O\Big(\dfrac{n}{2^{k}}\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

Proof.

We show each bound for the corresponding sum function separately. For all of the proofs below, let
U\geq|L|
; a symmetric result follows for
U<|L|
, which allows us to get the bounds stated in the theorem.

1.

We first show that

|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u)|\leq C_{n,k}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|u_{i}|\leq C_{n,k}\cdot nU,

where

C_{n,k}=\Big(1+\dfrac{1}{2^{k+1}}\Big)^{n-1}-1\approx\dfrac{n-1}{2^{k+1}}

for
n<2^{k}
. We proceed by induction on
n
. Hence assume that the proposition holds for
n-1
, and consider the addition step
\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)=\mathit{BS}^{*}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1})\oplus u_{n}
. Then,

|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u)|\leq|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-(\mathit{BS}^{*}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1})+u_{n})|+

|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1}+u_{n})-\mathit{BS}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1})+u_{n}|.

Let
s^{*}=\mathit{BS}^{*}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1}),s=\mathit{BS}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1})
. Then,

\displaystyle|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u)|

\displaystyle=|\mathit{BRound}(s^{*}+u_{n})-(s^{*}+u_{n})|+|s^{*}-s|

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{|s^{*}+u_{n}|}{2^{k+1}}+|s^{*}-s|

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{|s^{*}|+|u_{n}|}{2^{k+1}}+\Big(1+\dfrac{1}{2^{k+1}}\Big)\cdot|s^{*}-s|

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}|u_{i}|}{2^{k+1}}+\Big(1+\dfrac{1}{2^{k+1}}\Big)\cdot|s^{*}-s|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\dfrac{1}{2^{k+1}}+\Big(1+\dfrac{1}{2^{k+1}}\Big)C_{n-1}\Big)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|u_{i}|.

Since

C_{n}=\dfrac{1}{2^{k+1}}+\Big(1+\dfrac{1}{2^{k+1}}\Big)C_{n-1},

the induction step concludes. We remark that a similar accuracy bound is derived in
[
24
]
and
[
31
]
.

2.

We employ the accuracy bound shown in
[
24
]
. Let
t=1/2^{k+1}
(sometimes referred to as “unit roundoff” or “machine epsilon). Then,
[
24
, Eq. 3.6]
shows that

|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u)|\leq\dfrac{\log_{2}nt}{1-\log_{2}nt}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|v_{i}|.

In our setting, we have an upper bound on
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|v_{i}|
, and hence we can write

|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u)|\leq\dfrac{\log_{2}nt}{1-\log_{2}nt}\cdot nU.

3.

We employ the accuracy bound shown in
[
24
]
. Let
t=1/2^{k+1}
. Then,
[
24
, Eq. 3.11]
shows that

|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u)|\leq(2t+O(nt^{2}))\sum_{i=1}^{n}|v_{i}|

In our setting, we have an upper bound on
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|v_{i}|
(namely,
nU
), and hence we can write

|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-\mathit{BS}(u)|\leq(2t+O(nt^{2}))\cdot nU.

∎

Theorem 6.23

(Sensitivity upper bounds)
.

Let
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}:T^{n}_{[L,U]}\rightarrow T
be a bounded sum function on the normal
(k,\ell)
-bit floats of type
T
. Additionally, let
-U\leq L<U
. Then,

1.

If
\mathit{BS}^{*}
corresponds to iterative summation (Definition
5.1
), then

\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}\leq(U-L)+\Big(\dfrac{n^{2}}{2^{k}}\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

2.

If
\mathit{BS}^{*}
corresponds to pairwise summation (Definition
5.2
) and
n<2^{k}
, then

\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}\leq(U-L)+O\left(\dfrac{n\log n}{2^{k}}\right)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

3.

If
\mathit{BS}^{*}
corresponds to Kahan summation (Definition
5.3
) and
n<2^{k}
, then

\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}\leq(U-L)+O\Big(\dfrac{n}{2^{k}}\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

Proof.

The proof of each part follows from
Lemma

6.21
and the corresponding part of
Theorem

6.22
.
∎

In the cases where
n
is unknown, we can still make use of the upper bounds on the sensitivity that we showed in
Theorem

6.23
by introducing a truncation method. To that end, we introduce the definition of the
truncated bounded sum
method.

Method 6.24

(Truncated Bounded Sum)
.

Let
u
be a dataset of integers of type
T_{[L,U]}
,
U
an upper bound on the largest number in the dataset,
L
a lower bound on the smallest number in the dataset,
n_{\max}
a point at which to truncate the summation, and
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}
any summation method. Then, the
truncated bounded sum

\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}:\text{Vec}(T_{[L,U]})\to T
returns

•

\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)
if
\mathrm{len}(u)\leq n_{\max}
.

•

\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n_{\max}}]
if
\mathrm{len}(u)>n_{\max}
.

Theorem 6.25

(Idealized Sensitivity of Truncated Bounded Sum)
.

When the summation method
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}:\text{Vec}(T_{[L,U]})\to T
corresponds to the idealized bounded sum
\mathit{BS}_{L,U}
(Definition
4.2
), the truncated bounded sum
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}:\text{Vec}(T_{[L,U]})\to T
as defined in Method
6.24
has sensitivity
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}=\max\{|L|,U,U-L\}
.

Proof.

Consider all pairs of neighboring datasets
u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}
. There are two possible cases: (1) Truncation does not occur for either dataset. (2) Truncation occurs for at least one dataset.

1.

In case 1, we consider the standard bounded sum over a subset of all pairs of datasets
u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}
, so the idealized sensitivity can be bounded by
\max\{|L|,U\}
due to
Theorem

4.4
.

2.

In case 2, we consider the standard bounded sum over all pairs of datasets
u\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}
, where
u,u^{\prime}\in T^{n_{\max}}_{[L,U]}
, so the idealized sensitivity can be bounded by
U-L
due to
Theorem

4.4
.

Therefore,
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}\leq\max\{|L|,U,U-L\}
. We next prove that
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}\geq\max\{|L|,U,U-L\}
. Consider the datasets
u=[U_{1},\ldots,U_{n_{\max}},L]
and
u^{\prime}=[U_{1},\ldots,U_{n_{\max}-1},L]
. We see that
u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}
. We also see that
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}=U-L
, and
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}+1}=\max\{|L|,U\}
.

Therefore,
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}=\max\{|L|,U,U-L\}
.
∎

Corollary 6.26
.

Let
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}:\text{Vec}(T_{[L,U]})\rightarrow T
be the bounded sum function implemented with Method
6.24
on normal
(k,\ell)
floats. Then,

1.

If Method
6.24
is implemented with iterative summation, then

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(1+\dfrac{n_{\max}^{2}}{2^{k}}\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U,U-L\}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(2+\dfrac{n_{\max}^{2}}{2^{k}}\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

2.

If Method
6.24
is implemented with pairwise summation, then

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(1+O\left(\dfrac{n_{\max}\log n}{2^{k}}\right)\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U,U-L\}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(2+O\left(\dfrac{n_{\max}\log n}{2^{k}}\right)\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

3.

If Method
6.24
is implemented with Kahan summation, then

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n_{\max}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(1+O\Big(\dfrac{n_{\max}}{2^{k}}\Big)\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U,U-L\}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(2+O\Big(\dfrac{n_{\max}}{2^{k}}\Big)\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

Remark 6.27
.

We note that, where
U,L\geq 0
or
U,L\leq 0
, we have
U-L\leq\max\{|L|,U\}
, which gives us the following improvements on the sensitivity bounds provided in
Corollary

6.26
.

1.

If Method
6.24
is implemented with iterative summation, then

\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}\leq\Big(1+\dfrac{n_{\max}^{2}}{2^{k}}\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

2.

If Method
6.24
is implemented with pairwise summation, then

\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n_{\max}}\leq\Big(1+O\left(\dfrac{n_{\max}\log n}{2^{k}}\right)\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

3.

If Method
6.24
is implemented with Kahan summation, then

\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n_{\max}}\leq\Big(1+O\Big(\dfrac{n_{\max}}{2^{k}}\Big)\Big)\cdot\max\{|L|,U\}.

Note that, although
Corollary

6.26
is stated in terms of ordered sensitivities, pre-processing datasets with RP (
6.14
) will yield the same sensitivity bounds with respect to unordered distance metrics. Additionally, we can also employ a truncation strategy in the known
n
case if we want to establish an upper bound on the sensitivity of the implemented bounded sum function.

Data analysts should carefully choose
n_{\max}
. Because the implemented sensitivity grows with the length of the dataset, a larger
n_{\max}
means that the result will be noisier. However, if
n_{\max}\ll n
, causing many values of the dataset to be truncated, then the statistic may not be meaningful to the data analyst. Often, setting
n_{\max}
to an unrealistically large value can ensure that the sum is computed over the entire dataset while offering a minimal blow-up in the implemented sensitivity; for example, when working with 64-bit floats, the accuracy term will be less than 1 times the idealized sensitivity for
n_{\max}<67~\text{million}
(meaning that the majority of the implemented sensitivity will be attributable to the idealized sensitivity). In some cases, a noisy count can be helpful for choosing
n_{\max}
.

6.5.1
Shifting Bounds

We note that, when working in the bounded DP (known
n
) setting with
|U|,|L|\gg U-L
, the sensitivity provided in
Theorem

6.23
may be much larger than the idealized sensitivity
\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}=\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}=U-L
. (To reduce notation, the following discussion considers the case where
U,L>0
; a symmetric method can be used when
U,L<0
.) We can achieve a sensitivity much closer to the idealized sensitivity by subtracting
L
from every element of the dataset so that all elements lie in the interval
[L^{\prime}=0,U^{\prime}=U-L]
. We can then apply our solutions from
Section

6.5
, using 0 and
U^{\prime}
in the sensitivity bound, and adding
L\cdot n
at the end as post-processing. That is, we convert a method
\mathit{BS}^{*}
from
Section

6.5
into a method
\mathit{BS}^{**}
with sensitivity depending on
U-L
as follows:

\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L^{\prime},U^{\prime},n}(u)=\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(u_{1}-L,u_{2}-L,\ldots,u_{n}-L)+L\cdot n,

where a noisy version of the
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}
term is computed first and
L\cdot n
is added as a post-processing step.

Per
Corollary

6.26
, then, this results in a sensitivity of

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L^{\prime},U^{\prime},n_{\max}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(1+\dfrac{n_{\max}^{2}}{2^{k}}\Big)\cdot\max\{|L^{\prime}|,U^{\prime},U^{\prime}-L^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(1+\dfrac{n_{\max}^{2}}{2^{k}}\Big)\cdot(U-L).

6.6
Split Summation with Random Permutation

In this section, we present our method for a bounded sum function on floats that recovers sensitivity within a constant factor of the idealized sensitivity. While the attack presented in
Section

5.6
shows that the application of RP alone does not solve issues related to floating point arithmetic, we will show that we can achieve a good sensitivity for bounded sum by combining RP with our previous method of split summation and by changing the rounding mode from banker’s rounding to round toward 0 (see Definition
2.34
).

Round toward 0 allows us to achieve tighter bounds due to the property that, in many cases, the rounding never leads to an increase in the magnitude of the sum. We use this to argue that the sums of adjacent datasets rarely diverge. On the other hand – as we saw in the counterexamples in Sections
5.4
and
5.5
– banker’s rounding can make the magnitude of the sum increase much more quickly or much less quickly than expected, causing similar datasets to sometimes produce surprisingly dissimilar sums.

Method 6.28

(Split Summation on Floats)
.

Let
U
denote the upper bound,
L
the lower bound, and
u
the input dataset with elements of type
T
with round toward zero. We define
fsplit_sum
as follows:

⬇

1

def

fsplit_sum
(
u
)

2

P

=

0

3

N

=

0

4

for

elt

in

s
:

5

if

elt

<

0:

6

N

=

N

+

elt

7

else
:

8

P

=

P

+

elt

9

return

P

\oplus

N

There are two important features to note in the algorithm above.

1.

The iterative sums
N
and
P
are computed using round toward zero as defined in Definition
2.34
.

2.

The returned sum
P\oplus N
is computed using standard banker’s rounding as defined in Definition
2.33
. (This is the only use of banker’s rounding in the algorithm.)

Remark 6.29

(Avoiding Overflow)
.

Let
max
be the largest float
<\texttt{inf}
, and let
min
be the smallest float
>-\texttt{inf}
. To prevent overflow from occurring (which is necessary, as described in
Section

6.1.2
), we do the following. If
P=\texttt{inf}
, we set
P=\texttt{max}
, and if
N=-\texttt{inf}
, we set
N=\texttt{min}
. Issues related to non-associativity will not arise because we are using split summation and issues of non-associativity do not affect split summation (see
Section

6.3
).

We now state a theorem about the sensitivity of the function described in
6.28
.

Theorem 6.30

(
\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}
and
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}
of
6.28
)
.

Let
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}:\text{Vec}(T)\rightarrow T
be the bounded sum function with split summation and round toward zero (
6.28
) on the normal
(k,\ell)
-bit floats of type
T
, and similarly for
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}:T^{n}\rightarrow T
. Define
f_{L,U}=\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}\circ\mathit{RP}
and
f_{L,U,n}=\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}\circ\mathit{RP}
. Then,

1.

(Unknown
n
.) Let
b_{U}=\lfloor\log_{2}U\rfloor,b_{L}=\lfloor\log_{2}|L|\rfloor
.
Then,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}f_{L,U}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{2^{b_{U}}+U,2^{b_{L}}+|L|\}+\max\{2^{b_{U}},2^{b_{L}}\}

\displaystyle\leq 3\cdot\max\{U,|L|\}.

2.

(Known
n
.) Let
c_{U}=\lfloor\log_{2}U\rfloor
,
b_{U}=\min\{c_{U},\lfloor\log_{2}(nU)\rfloor-k\}
,

c_{L}=\lfloor\log_{2}|L|\rfloor
,
b_{L}=\min\{c_{L},\lfloor\log_{2}(|nL|\rfloor-k\}
.

Then,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}f_{L,U,n}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{b_{U}}+U+2^{b_{L}}+|L|

\displaystyle+\max\{\min\{2^{b_{U}},2^{c_{U}+1}\},\min\{2^{b_{L}},2^{c_{L}+1}\}\}

\displaystyle\leq 5\cdot\max\{U,|L|\}.

Recall, from Theorem
6.19
for unknown dataset length and Corollary
6.20
for known dataset length, that RP allows us to reason about the ordered distance between datasets, even when we are only provided with a guarantee about the unordered distance between the datasets. For this reason, we state a theorem about the sensitivities of
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}
and
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}
with respect to ordered distance metrics, since sensitivities with respect to ordered distance metrics will be able to be converted to statements about sensitivities with respect to unordered distances.

Theorem 6.31

(Ordered Sensitivity of Split Summation with RP for Floats)
.

Let
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}:\text{Vec}(T)\rightarrow T
be the bounded sum function with split summation and round toward zero (
6.28
) on the normal
(k,\ell)
-bit floats of type
T
, and similarly for
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}:T^{n}\rightarrow T
. Then,

1.

(Unknown
n
.) Let
b_{U}=\lfloor\log_{2}U\rfloor,b_{L}=\lfloor\log_{2}|L|\rfloor
.
Then,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{2^{b_{U}}+U,2^{b_{L}}+|L|\}+\max\{2^{b_{U}},2^{b_{L}}\}

\displaystyle\leq 3\cdot\max\{U,|L|\}.

2.

(Known
n
.) Let
c_{U}=\lfloor\log_{2}U\rfloor
,
b_{U}=\min\{c_{U},\lfloor\log_{2}(nU)\rfloor-k\}
,

c_{L}=\lfloor\log_{2}|L|\rfloor
,
b_{L}=\min\{c_{L},\lfloor\log_{2}(|nL|\rfloor-k\}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{b_{U}}+U+2^{b_{L}}+|L|+\max\{\min\{2^{b_{U}},2^{c_{U}+1}\},

\displaystyle\min\{2^{b_{L}},2^{c_{L}+1}\}\}

\displaystyle\leq 5\cdot\max\{U,|L|\}.

Before proceeding with the proof of Theorem
6.31
, we state and prove some lemmas about the behavior of floating point arithmetic. The motivation for these lemmas is to determine the situations in which rounding can cause the iterative sums of adjacent datasets to diverge. Specifically, we prove that this divergence-causing rounding only occurs when the intermediate sum moves from a floating point interval of the form
[2^{k},2^{k+1})
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
to a floating point interval of the form
[2^{\ell},2^{\ell+1})
where
k\neq\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 6.32

(Distance Between Sums – Part 1)
.

Let
s,s^{\prime},v
be
(k,\ell)
-bit floats such that
\textit{sign}(s)=\textit{sign}(s^{\prime})=\textit{sign}(v)
,
|s^{\prime}|\geq|s|
, and
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s+v)=\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)
. Then it follows that
|\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)-\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)|\leq|s-s^{\prime}|
.

Proof.

We present the proof for the case that
s,s^{\prime},v
are all positive; the case in which they are all negative is similar. We first consider
\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)
.

\displaystyle\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)

\displaystyle=\Big\lfloor\frac{s+v}{\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s+v)}\Big\rfloor\cdot\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s+v)

\displaystyle=\Big\lfloor\frac{s+v}{\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)}\Big\rfloor\cdot\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)

\displaystyle=s+\Big\lfloor\frac{v}{\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)}\Big\rfloor\cdot\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s),

with the final equality following from the fact that
s
is an integer multiple of
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)
.

We now consider
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)
.

\displaystyle\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)

\displaystyle=\Big\lfloor\frac{s^{\prime}+v}{\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s^{\prime}+v)}\Big\rfloor\cdot\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s+v)

\displaystyle\leq s^{\prime}+\Big\lfloor\frac{v}{\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s^{\prime}+v)}\Big\rfloor\cdot\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s^{\prime}+v)

\displaystyle\leq s^{\prime}+\Big\lfloor\frac{v}{\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)}\Big\rfloor\cdot\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s).

Therefore, we see that
|\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)-\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)|\leq|s-s^{\prime}|
.
∎

Observe that Lemma
6.32
is not affected by
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v))
. This is because the spacing between NRFs increases in each interval, so adding
v
to
s^{\prime}
will not have a larger effect RTZ on the sum than adding
v
to
s
, where
|s^{\prime}|>|s|
. Note that this does not hold if banker’s rounding were used instead of round toward zero.

Lemma 6.33

(Distance Between Sums – Part 2)
.

If two
(k,\ell)
-bit floats
s,s^{\prime}\geq 0
are such that
s<s^{\prime}
(with
s^{\prime}-s=d
), and we add a term
v>0
to both terms such that
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s+v)=2\cdot\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)
, it follows that
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)-\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)\leq d+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s).

Proof.

Let
s\in[2^{k+m},2^{k+m})
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, so
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)=\frac{2^{k+m}}{2^{k+m}}=2^{m}
. Also, suppose that
v\in[p\cdot 2^{m},p\cdot 2^{m+1})
for some
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, and that
2^{k+m+1}-s=q\cdot 2^{m}
for some
q\in\mathbb{Z}
. Additionally, suppose that
s^{\prime}\geq 2^{k+m+1}
.

We now note that
\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)=s+q\cdot 2^{m}+\lfloor\frac{p-q}{2}\rfloor\cdot 2^{m+1}
. We proceed with a proof by cases.

1.

Suppose that
p
is even, but that
(p-q)
is odd. This means, then, that computing
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)
will yield
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)\leq s^{\prime}+\frac{p}{2}\cdot 2^{m+1}=s^{\prime}+p\cdot 2^{m}
. On the other hand, computing
\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)
will result in a value of
\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)=s+q\cdot 2^{m}+\frac{p-q-1}{2}\cdot 2^{m+1}=s+q\cdot 2^{m}+(p-q-1)\cdot 2^{m}=s+(p-1)\cdot 2^{m}
.

In this case, then,
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)-\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)\leq d+2^{k}=d+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)
.

2.

Now, suppose that
p
is even and
(p-q)
is even. This means, then, that computing
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)
will yield
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)\leq s^{\prime}+\frac{p}{2}\cdot 2^{m+1}=s^{\prime}+p\cdot 2^{m}
. On the other hand, computing
\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)
will result in a value of
\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)=s+q\cdot 2^{m}+\frac{p-q}{2}\cdot 2^{m+1}=s+p\cdot 2^{m}
.

In this case, then,
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)-\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)\leq d
.

3.

Finally, suppose that
p
is odd. This means, then, that computing
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)
will yield
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)\leq\frac{p-1}{2}\cdot 2^{m+1}=s^{\prime}+(p-1)\cdot 2^{m}
. On the other hand, computing
\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)
will result in a value of
\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)=s+q\cdot 2^{m}+\lfloor\frac{p-q}{2}\rfloor\cdot 2^{m+1}\geq s+q\cdot 2^{m}+(p-q-1)\cdot 2^{m}=s+(p-1)\cdot 2^{m}.

In this case, then,
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)-\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)\leq d
.

Note that, in the cases above, we only considered
s^{\prime}\geq 2^{k+m+1}
. We now consider the case where
s,s^{\prime}\in[2^{k+m},2^{k+m+1})
. Let
q^{\prime}
be the corresponding “
q
value” for
s^{\prime}
. The worst case arises when
(p-q)
is odd and
(p-q^{\prime})
is even. We then see the behavior provided in case (1), where
p
is even but
(p-q)
is odd, so the bounds on the difference as provided in the theorem statement still hold.

Therefore,
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)-\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)\leq d+2^{m}=d+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)
.

∎

Corollary 6.34
.

If two
(k,\ell)
-bit floats
s,s^{\prime}\geq 0
are such that
s<s^{\prime}
(with
s^{\prime}-s=d
), and we add a term
v>0
to both terms such that
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(\mathit{RTZ}(s+v))=2^{m}\cdot\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, it follows that
\mathit{RTZ}(s^{\prime}+v)-\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)\leq d+(2^{m}\cdot\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)-\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(s)).

Before proceeding to the next lemma, we introduce a new term.

Definition 6.35

(Boundary)
.

We refer to a value of the form
2^{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
as a
boundary
. This is because, for values
s\in[2^{m},2^{m+1})
, the distance between NRFs is twice that of the distance between NRFs for values
s\in[2^{m-1},2^{m})
. Whenever we have a floating point value
s\in[2^{m},2^{m+1})
, and we add a value
v
where
\mathit{RTZ}(s+v)\in(2^{m+j},2^{m+j+1})
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we say that the sum
crossed a boundary
.

Lemma 6.36

(Maximum Boundary Crossing Given
U
)
.

Let
U
be the upper bound for the bounded sum function over the
(k,\ell)
-bit floats, and let
b\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
U\in[2^{b},2^{b+1})
. Then, in the worst case, the final crossed boundary corresponds to the boundary
2^{k+b}
, between the intervals
[2^{k+b-1},2^{k+b})
and
[2^{k+b},2^{k+b+1})
.

Proof.

Under round toward zero, the maximum sum that a dataset can reach with
U\in[2^{b},2^{b+1})
is
2^{b+1}\cdot 2^{k}
. Therefore, the final boundary that we cross is no greater than the boundary between the intervals
[2^{k+b-1},2^{k+b})
and
[2^{k+b},2^{k+b+1})
. Note that, because the maximum sum is
2^{k+b+1}
, we have not yet
crossed
into the interval
(2^{k+b+1},2^{k+b+2})
.

∎

Lemma 6.37

(Maximum Boundary Crossing Given
U,n
)
.

Let
U
be the upper bound for the bounded sum function over the
(k,\ell)
-bit floats, let
n
be the length of the dataset, and let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
U\cdot n\in[2^{m},2^{m+1})
. Then, in the worst case, the final crossed boundary corresponds to the boundary
2^{m}
, between the intervals
[2^{m-1},2^{m})
and
[2^{m},2^{m+1})
.

Proof.

Since we are in the round-toward-zero rounding mode, the maximum sum of a length
n
dataset with upper bound
U
is
n\cdot U
. Because we define
m\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
U\cdot n\in[2^{m},2^{m+1})
and
U\cdot n\in[2^{m},2^{m+1})
, we see that
2^{m}
is the largest boundary.
∎

Having developed lemmas about the behavior of floating point arithmetic under round toward 0, we are now ready to reason about the sensitivity of the bounded sum function described in Method
6.28
.

We remark that the intervals stated in Lemma
6.36
are a worst-case scenario, and thus are not necessarily reached by the sum value.

Lemma 6.38

(Sensitivity
\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U,n}
)
.

Suppose we have two length
n
datasets
u\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}
of
(k,\ell)
-bit floats, with values clipped within the interval
[0,U]
with
U\in[2^{c},2^{c+1})
, where the maximum possible sum (in this case,
\min\{n\cdot U,2^{k+c}\}
) is
\in[2^{k+b},2^{k+b+1})
. Then, the maximum difference in sums will be

\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U,n}\leq\sum_{i=-\infty}^{k+b-1}\frac{2^{i}}{2^{k}}+U=2^{b}+U\leq 2U,

where
2^{b}\leq\min\{U,\frac{n\cdot U}{2^{k}}\}
.

Proof.

Let
u\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}
be two length
n
datasets of
(k,\ell)
-bit floats.
In the worst case, this means that
u
and
u^{\prime}
are identical, except they differ at some value
u_{i}\neq u^{\prime}_{i}
. In the worst case, without loss of generality,
u_{i}=0
and
u^{\prime}_{i}=U
.

We note that we have
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}([u^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{i}])-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}([u_{1},\ldots,u_{i}])\leq U
.

By Lemma
6.32
, if for all
j\geq i
and some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}([u_{1},\ldots,u_{j-1}]),\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}([u_{1},\ldots,u_{j}])\in[2^{m},2^{m+1}),

then we will continue to have

\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}([u^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{j}])-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}([u_{1},\ldots,u_{j}])\leq U.

However, we may have
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}([u_{1},\ldots,u_{j-1}])\in[2^{m},2^{m+1})
but
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}([u_{1},\ldots,u_{j}])\not\in[2^{m},2^{m+1})
. In this case, we apply Lemma
6.33
. This tells us that the rounding that causes the difference in sums to grow only occurs when adding a value to the smaller sum (in this case, adding
u_{j}
) causes the sum to cross from an interval of values in
[2^{m},2^{m+1})
to an interval of values in
[2^{p},2^{p+1})
for some
p\in\mathbb{Z}>m
. By Lemma
6.32
, the difference in sums does
not
grow when the smaller sum does
not
cross from one interval into another interval. Therefore, we only experience growth in the difference of sums (recall that the “true” difference is
\mathit{BS}_{0,U}(u^{\prime})-\mathit{BS}_{0,U}(u)=U
, so we expect this much of a difference in sums) when these interval crossings occur.

Suppose we are crossing from a value
r
with
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(r)=2^{q}
to a value
r^{\prime}
with
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(r^{\prime})=2^{q+1}
. By Lemma
6.33
, we experience growth in the difference in sums of at most
2^{q}
.

By Lemma
6.36
, the maximum round-up occurs when crossing from an interval with a
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}
of
2^{b-2}
to an interval with a
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}
of
2^{b-1}
. Therefore, the greatest growth in the difference in sums that we can experience is
2^{b-1}
. We note that the crossing immediately prior to this can result in a growth in the difference in sums of
2^{b-2}
. Therefore, we can bound the overall growth in difference in sums by
2^{b-1}+2^{b-2}+2^{b-3}+\ldots=\sum_{m=-\infty}^{b-1}2^{m}=\sum_{m=-\infty}^{k+b-1}\frac{2^{m}}{2^{k}}
. Because
m
ranges from
-\infty
to
(k+b-1)
, the growth in the difference in sums is bounded by
2\cdot 2^{b-1}=2^{b}
, which is turn bounded by
U
given that, by assumption,
U<2^{b+1}
. (Note that the bound provided in Corollary
6.34
would have a smaller effect than the roundings at every boundary crossing that we have here, so we only consider the case described in Lemma
6.33
when evaluating worst-case behavior.)

Therefore, the overall difference in sums for two datasets with difference in sums
|\mathit{BS}_{L,U}(u)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U}(u^{\prime})|\leq U
is at most the bound provided in the lemma statement,

|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U,n}(u)-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U,n}(u^{\prime})|\leq\sum_{i=-\infty}^{k+b-1}\frac{2^{i}}{2^{k}}+U=2^{b}+U\leq 2U.

∎

Corollary 6.39

(Sensitivity
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}
)
.

Suppose we have two datasets
u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}
of
(k,\ell)
-bit floats, with values clipped to the interval
[0,U]
and with
U\in[2^{b},2^{b+1})
, meaning that the maximum possible sum is
\in[2^{k+b},2^{k+b+1})
. Then, the maximum difference in sums will be

\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}\leq\sum_{i=-\infty}^{k+b-1}\frac{2^{i}}{2^{k}}+U=2^{b}+U\leq 2U.

Proof.

Let
u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}
be two datasets of
(k,\ell)
-bit floats. Without loss of generality, let
\mathrm{len}(u)<\mathrm{len}(u^{\prime})
. Let
u^{\prime\prime}
be the dataset that results from inserting a 0 immediately before the first location
u_{i}
where we have
u_{i}\neq u^{\prime}_{i}
. (Because 0’s have no effect on a floating point summation, this insertion will not affect the difference in sums.)

We now note that
u^{\prime\prime}\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}
, so we can apply Lemma
6.38
. Because
n
is not known, we can pretend that we have
n=\infty
, which causes the “
\min
” in the lemma statement to evaluate to
2^{k+b}
. (We could not actually have
n=\infty
since computers are finite, but this helps evaluate the worst case value for the “
\min
” term.) Using this lemma and by the definition of sensitivity, we have

|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U,n}(u^{\prime\prime})-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U,n}(u^{\prime})|\leq\sum_{i=-\infty}^{k+b-1}\frac{2^{i}}{2^{k}}+d=2^{b}+d\leq 2U.

Because of how we constructed
u^{\prime\prime}
, we equivalently have

|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}(u)-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}(u^{\prime})|\leq\sum_{i=-\infty}^{k+b-1}\frac{2^{i}}{2^{k}}+d=2^{b}+d\leq 2U.

We know that we have
u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}
, so by the definition of sensitivity, we have

\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{0,U}\leq\sum_{i=-\infty}^{k+b-1}\frac{2^{i}}{2^{k}}+d=2^{b}+d\leq 2U.

∎

Proof of Theorem
6.31
.

Recall that we are working with the bounded sum function as described in Method
6.28
. Additionally, note that we prove (1)
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}
and (2)
\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}
.

1.

Let
u\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}
be two datasets of length
n
with
(k,\ell)
-bit floating point values of type
T
. In the worst case, this means that
u
and
u^{\prime}
are identical, except they differ at some value
u_{i}\neq u^{\prime}_{i}
.

By Corollary
6.39
, where
U\in[2^{b},2^{b+1})
, the calculation of
P
has a sensitivity of at most
2^{b}+U
. By generalizing Corollary
6.39
to work with values in
[L,0]
instead, it follows by symmetry that, where
L\in(-2^{b^{\prime}+1},-2^{b^{\prime}}]
, the calculation of
N
has a sensitivity of at most
2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|
.

The last step of Method
6.28
consists of adding
P
and
N
using banker’s rounding. We need to account for the rounding that can occur in this step. Let
M_{P}
be the maximum value of
P
, and let
M_{N}
be the minimum value (i.e., maximum magnitude) of
N
. We see, then, that
M_{P}=2^{b}\cdot 2^{k+1}
, and that
M_{N}=-2^{b^{\prime}}\cdot 2^{k+1}
.

Suppose that
M_{P}\geq|M_{N}|
. In the worst case, for all values
v<M_{P}
, we have
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(v)\leq 2^{b}
, so the worst-case round-down that we can get from combining
P
and
N
(using banker’s rounding) is
\frac{2^{b}}{2}
. Likewise, the worst-case round-up that we can get from combining
P
and
N
(using banker’s rounding) is
\frac{2^{b}}{2}
. Therefore, the worst-case overall rounding is
2^{b}
. Likewise, for the
M_{P}\leq|M_{N}|
case, the worst-case rounding is
2^{b^{\prime}}
.

We recall that the sensitivity for computing
N
is
\leq 2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|
; and that the sensitivity for
P
is
\leq 2^{b}+U
. Therefore, the overall sensitivity initially appears to be

\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}\leq 2^{b}+U+2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|+\max\{2^{b},2^{b^{\prime}}\}.

We can improve on this upper bound on the sensitivity, though, with the observation that we have
d_{\mathit{ID}}(s,s^{\prime})=1
. A single insertion or a single deletion will only affect one of
M_{P}
or
M_{N}
(whereas the sensitivity above assumes that both
M_{P}
and
M_{N}
would be affected). In the worst case, the single insertion or deletion would affect the value that causes maximum rounding. Therefore, we get

\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}\leq\max\{2^{b}+U,2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|\}+\max\{2^{b},2^{b^{\prime}}\}.

For
U\geq|L|
, then, the overall sensitivity is
\leq 3U
, and for
U<|L|
, the overall sensitivity is
\leq 3\cdot|L|
. The overall sensitivity, then, can be upper-bounded by
3\cdot\max\{U,|L|\}
.

2.

Let
u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}
be two datasets with
(k,\ell)
-bit floating-point values of type
T
. Without loss of generality, let
\mathrm{len}(u)<\mathrm{len}(u^{\prime})
.

By Lemma
6.38
, where
U\in[2^{c},2^{c+1})
, and where
\min\{n\cdot U,2^{k+c}\}\in[2^{k+b},2^{k+b+1})
, the calculation of
P
has a sensitivity of at most
2^{b}+U
. By generalizing Lemma
6.38
to work with values in
[L,0]
instead, it follows by symmetry that, where
L\in(-2^{c^{\prime}+1},-2^{c^{\prime}}]
, and where
\min\{|n\cdot L|,2^{k+c^{\prime}}\}\in(-2^{k+b^{\prime}+1},-2^{k+b^{\prime}}]
, the calculation of
N
has a sensitivity of at most
2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|
.

The last step of Method
6.28
consists of adding
P
and
N
using banker’s rounding. We need to account for the rounding that can occur in this step.

If
n\cdot U\geq 2^{k+c}
, then – by the reasoning in (1) – the maximum rounding effect is
2^{b}
when
M_{P}\geq|M_{L}|
. Likewise, if
|n\cdot L|\geq 2^{k+c^{\prime}}
, then – by the reasoning in (1) – the maximum rounding effect is
2^{b^{\prime}}
when
M_{P}<|M_{L}|
.

We now consider the case where
n\cdot U<2^{k+c}
, and we also consider the case where
|n\cdot L|\geq 2^{k+c^{\prime}}
.

Let
M_{P}
be the maximum value of
P
, and let
M_{N}
be the minimum value (i.e., maximum magnitude) of
N
. We see, then, that
M_{P}\leq 2^{c+1}\cdot 2^{k}
, and that
|M_{N}|\leq|-2^{c^{\prime}+1}\cdot 2^{k}|
.

Suppose that
M_{P}\geq|M_{N}|
. In the worst case,
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(M_{P})=2^{c+1}
, so the worst-case round-down that we can get from combining
P
and
N
(using banker’s rounding) is
\frac{2^{c+1}}{2}
. Likewise, the worst-case round-up that we can get from combining
P
and
N
(using banker’s rounding) is
\frac{2^{c+1}}{2}
. Therefore, the worst-case overall rounding is
2^{c+1}
. Likewise, for the
M_{P}\leq|M_{N}|
case, the worst-case rounding is
2^{c^{\prime}+1}
.

We recall that the sensitivity for computing
N
is
\leq 2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|
; and that the sensitivity for
P
is
\leq 2^{b}+U
. Therefore, the overall sensitivity is

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{b}+U+2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|+\max\{\min\{2^{b},2^{c+1}\},\min\{2^{b^{\prime}},2^{c^{\prime}+1}\}\}.

For
U\geq|L|
, then, the overall sensitivity is
\leq 5U
, and for
U<|L|
, the overall sensitivity is
\leq 5L
. The overall sensitivity, then, can be upper-bounded by
5\cdot\max\{U,|L|\}
.

∎

Having proven sensitivities with respect to ordered distance metrics, we now prove sensitivities with respect to unordered distance metrics.

Proof of Theorem
6.30
.

Recall that we are working with the bounded sum function as described in the theorem statement. Additionally, note that we prove (1)
\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}
and (2)
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}
.

1.

We first apply RP to the input dataset, and Theorem
6.19
tells us that RP transforms datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{Sym}}
to a coupling of datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{ID}}
. We can now work with datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{ID}}
. Theorem
6.31
tells us that

\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}\leq\max\{2^{b}+U,2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|\}+\max\{2^{b},2^{b^{\prime}}\},

so we also have

\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}\leq\max\{2^{b}+U,2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|\}+\max\{2^{b},2^{b^{\prime}}\},

completing the proof.

2.

We first apply RP to the input dataset, and Corollary
6.20
tells us that RP transforms datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{CO}}
to a coupling of datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{Ham}}
. We can now work with datasets that are neighboring with respect to
d_{\mathit{Ham}}
. Theorem
6.31
tells us that

\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}\leq 2^{b}+U+2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|+\max\{\min\{2^{b},2^{c+1}\},\min\{2^{b^{\prime}},2^{c^{\prime}+1}\}\},

so we also have

\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}\leq 2^{b}+U+2^{b^{\prime}}+|L|+\max\{\min\{2^{b},2^{c+1}\},\min\{2^{b^{\prime}},2^{c^{\prime}+1}\}\},

completing the proof.

∎

6.7
Reducing Floats to Integers

Another attractive solution for floating-point summation is to reduce it to integer summation, since the latter achieves the idealized
sensitivity with simple solutions. The idea behind this method is to cast floats to fixed-point numbers, which we can think of as
k
-bit integers. To achieve this casting, we introduce a discretization parameter
D
(which is chosen by the data analyst), round each of the dataset elements according to the discretized interval, and apply one of our integer solutions. We can think of this process of “integerizing” as a dataset transform.

We first choose a discretization parameter
D
(we discuss how to make this choice below) and set
M
as a function of
L
and
U
(if
|U|\geq|L|
, we set
M=\max\{L,0\}
; otherwise, we set
M=\min\{U,0\}
) to shift the discretization range to better take advantage of the range of signed integers and obtain accuracy that is better than a no-shift strategy. We consider the function mapping a dataset

u=[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}]
of floats to the signed integer dataset

\mathit{Float2Int}_{L,U,D}(u)=[\mathit{round}((u_{1}-M)/D),\ldots,\mathit{round}((u_{n}-M)/D)],

(8)

where
\mathit{round}(\cdot)
denotes rounding to the nearest integer (we explore various implementations of the
\mathit{round}(\cdot)
function in Definitions
6.45
and
6.46
and explore their impacts throughout the accuracy theorems in this section).

Method 6.40

(Reducing floats to integers, bounded DP case)
.

Let
L
denote the lower bound,
U
the upper bound,
u=[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}]
the input dataset of normal
(k,\ell)
-bit floats, and
D
the discretization parameter (where
L,U,D
are
(k,\ell)
-bit normal floats). We additionally require that
|U|\geq|L|
, though symmetric results hold for
|L|>|U|
.

Let
M=\max\{L,0\}
, and let
K=\lceil(U-M)/D\rceil
and
J=\lfloor(L-M)/D\rfloor
. Then, the reducing floats to ints method is as follows:

1.

Compute

\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)=(\mathit{BS}^{*}_{J,K,n}\circ\mathit{Float2Int}_{L,U,D,n})(u),

where
\mathit{BS}^{*}
is any one of the integer-summation methods discussed in Section
6.1.1
,
6.3
, or
6.4
.
21
21

21

This method can be adapted to work with the modular summation described in
Section

6.2
, too, though the accuracy guarantees may not hold.

2.

Perform noise addition to obtain the DP mechanism

\mathcal{M}_{L,U,D,n}(u)=\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)+\mathit{Noise}((K-J)/\varepsilon),

where
\mathit{Noise}(s)
denotes a noise distribution with scale
s
suitable for
k
-bit integers (e.g., the discrete Laplace Mechanism
[
20
]
).

3.

Rescale and shift the resulting number to obtain the final floating-point result

\mathcal{M}_{L,U,D,n}(u)\cdot D+M\cdot n.

Note that this method can be adapted to the setting in which the length of the dataset is not known by using the truncation technique (and associated sensitivities) in
6.24
.

Remark 6.41
.

The requirement that
|U|\geq|L|
is not restrictive. Indeed, if
|L|>|U|
, then we apply Method
6.40
with
M=\min\{U,0\}
instead of
M=\max\{L,0\}
.

Theorem 6.42

(Sensitivity of
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}
)
.

Let
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}
be as defined in
6.40
. The sensitivity of this function is
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}=\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{J,K,n}\leq K-J
, where
K=\left\lceil(U-M)/D\right\rceil
and
J=\left\lfloor(L-M)/D\right\rfloor
.

Proof.

Let
u,v
be two datasets of length
n
of normal
(k,\ell)
-bit floats. Then, we claim that

d_{\mathit{CO}}(\mathit{Float2Int}_{L,U,D}(u),\mathit{Float2Int}_{L,U,D}(v))\leq d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v).

Indeed, since by definition the
\mathit{Float2Int}
function subtracts
M
from each element and divides the result by
D
, for any elements
u_{j},v_{k}
such that
u_{j}=v_{k}
, it follows that
\mathit{round}((u_{j}-M)/D)=\mathit{round}((v_{j}-M/D)
. For any pair
u_{j},v_{k}
such that
u_{j}\neq v_{k}
, either
\mathit{round}((u_{j}-M)/D)\neq\mathit{round}((v_{k}-M)/D)
or
\mathit{round}((u_{j}-M)/D)=\mathit{round}((v_{k}-M)/D)
due to rounding errors. Either way, the minimum number of elements in
\mathit{Float2Int}(u)
that need to be changed to produce
\mathit{Float2Int}(v)
can only be less than the minimum number of elements in
u
that need to be changed to produce
v
.

Moreover, the smallest value produced by this operation is
\geq J
, and the largest value produced by this operation is
\leq K
, so
J
and
K
are correct lower and upper bounds, respectively, on the elements in our transformed dataset. Therefore, the sensitivity is
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}\leq K-J
.

∎

Corollary 6.43
.

\mathcal{M}_{L,U,D,n}
satisfies
\epsilon
-DP.

Proof.

By
Theorem

6.42
, the sensitivity of
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}
is
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}=(K-J)
so, because scaling our noise distribution to
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}/\varepsilon
satisfies
\varepsilon
-DP, it follows that
\mathcal{M}_{L,U,D,n}
satisfies
\varepsilon
-DP.
∎

Corollary 6.44
.

6.40
satisfies
\varepsilon
-DP.

Proof.

By
Corollary

6.43
,
\mathcal{M}_{L,U,D,n}
satisfies
\varepsilon
-DP. Moreover, that
D,M,n
are all non-private values. Step 3 of
6.40
, therefore, just post-processes a result that satisfies
\varepsilon
-DP. Therefore, because DP is robust to post-processing (
Proposition

2.18
), Step 3 satisfies
\varepsilon
-DP and
6.40
satisfies
\varepsilon
-DP.
∎

In order to analyze the accuracy of
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}
, we need to set the
round(\cdot)
function to a concrete rounding mode. We consider the following two rounding modes.

Definition 6.45

(Round to nearest integer)
.

For a value
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
round to nearest integer
maps
x
to the integer
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that, for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
|x-x^{\prime}|<|x-y|
.

Definition 6.46

(Randomized round to nearest integer)
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
,
y\in\mathbb{Z}
be the largest integer such that
y<x
, and
z\in\mathbb{Z}
the smallest integer such that
x<z
.
Randomized round to nearest integer
maps
x
to
y
with probability
x-y
, and maps
x
to
z
otherwise.

Theorem 6.47

(Accuracy of
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}
under round to nearest)
.

When the
round(\cdot)
function in
\mathit{Float2Int}
(Equation
8
) corresponds to round to nearest integer (
Definition

6.45
), then

|\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)-(\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)-M\cdot n)/D|\leq n/2.

Proof.

Since two consecutive integers are at distance 1, each term
u_{i}
in the dataset is rounded to a value within 1/2 of the true value
u_{i}/D
. Because
u
is of length
n
, the total value of the sum can be rounded to a value within
n/2
of the true value, which yields the above accuracy.
∎

Theorem 6.48

(Accuracy of
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}
under randomized rounding)
.

When the
round(\cdot)
function in
\mathit{Float2Int}
(Equation
8
) corresponds to randomized round to nearest integer (
Definition

6.46
), then with probability
1-2/e^{2c^{2}}
for all
c>0
,

|\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)-(\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)-M\cdot n)/D|\leq c\sqrt{n}=O(\sqrt{n}).

Proof.

We use Hoeffding bounds. Note that, due to the use of randomized rounding,
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)
is a random variable. We can write
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)=\sum_{i\in[n]}(u_{i}-M)/D.
We see that
u_{i}-M
is exactly representable as a floating-point number. We also note that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{E}[\mathit{round}(x/D)]=x/D
. Therefore,
\mathbb{E}[\mathit{round}((u_{i}-M)/D)]=(u_{i}-M)/D
. Therefore,
\mathbb{E}[\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)]=\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)-M\cdot n
.

We now apply Hoeffding bounds to
\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)
. For all
c\in\mathbb{R}
where
c>0
, we have

\Pr[|\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)-(\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)-M\cdot n)/D|\geq c\sqrt{n}]\leq 2\exp(-2c^2).

∎

Next we prove that overflow in Step 2 of
6.40
is very unlikely. Note that if (floating-point) overflow occurs in Step 3, this means that the user was dealing with a dataset with very large values (i.e., close to the maximum representable floating-point exponent) that are unlikely to result in an accurate answer regardless of the summation strategy.

Theorem 6.49

(Overflow is unlikely)
.

Let the discretization parameter
D
in Method
6.40
be

D=(U-M)\cdot n/(2^{m-2}),

the
\mathit{Noise}
term in Step 2 added from the discrete Laplace mechanism, and let
\mathit{Float2Int}
cast to
m
-bit signed integers. Then, the probability of overflow in the computation of
\mathcal{M}_{L,U,D,n}(u)
is

\exp(-\Omega(K\cdot n/(K/\varepsilon)))=\exp(-\Omega(\eps\cdot n)).

Proof.

By Step 2 in Method
6.40
, we draw noise with scale
(K-J)/\epsilon\leq 2K/\epsilon
. We know that the geometric mechanism has PDF
f(x)\propto\exp(-\epsilon\cdot|x| / 2)

[
3
]
.

Let
X\sim\mathit{Noise}((K-J)/\epsilon)
. We are interested in the probability of overflow – that is, we are interested in the probability that
K\cdot n+X\geq 2^{m-1}
. Using the CDF of the geometric mechanism, we see that

\displaystyle\Pr[|K\cdot n+X|\geq 2^{m-1}]

\displaystyle=\exp( -\Omega((2^{m-1} - Kn)/((K-J)/\epsilon) ))

\displaystyle=\exp( -\Omega((2^{m-1} - Kn)/(2K/\epsilon) ))

\displaystyle=\exp( -\Omega(Kn/(2K/\epsilon) ))

\displaystyle=\exp( -\Omega(n \epsilon)),

with the third equality following from the fact that
2^{m-1}-Kn>Kn
.

∎

Theorem 6.50
.

Set the discretization parameter
D
in Method
6.40
to be

D=(U-M)\cdot n/2^{m-2},

where we operate with normal
(k,\ell)
-bit floats. Then:

•

If the rounding mode in
\mathit{Float2Int}
corresponds to round to nearest,

\displaystyle|(\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n}(u)\cdot D+M\cdot n)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)|\leq\dfrac{D}{n^{k}}+\dfrac{Mn}{2^{k+1}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(U-M)n}{2^{m+k-2}}+\dfrac{Mn}{2^{k+1}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{U\cdot n^{2}}{2^{k+1}}\right),

where all the operations in the first set of parentheses are done with floating-point values using banker’s rounding.

•

If the rounding mode in
\mathit{Float2Int}
corresponds to randomized randomized round to nearest,

\displaystyle|(\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,n}(u)\cdot D)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)|\leq O\Big(\dfrac{D\sqrt{n}+Mn}{2^{k+1}}\Big)

\displaystyle=O\Big(\dfrac{(U-M)n^{3/2}}{2^{m+k-1}}+\dfrac{Mn}{2^{k+1}}\Big)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{U\cdot n^{3/2}}{2^{k+1}}\right)

with high probability, where all the operations in the first set of parentheses are done with floating-point values using banker’s rounding.

Proof.

For the first inequality, by Theorem
6.47
it follows that

|\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)/D|\leq n/2.

To obtain the term
(\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)\cdot D+M\cdot n)
, we multiply by
D
and obtain a floating point number. This yields the
Dn/2^{k}
term in the RHS. Similarly, the term
Mn
requires multiplying
M
and
n
together to obtain a floating point number, which yields the
Mn/2^{k+1}
term in the RHS. Hence by setting
D
as above the result follows.

For the second inequality, by Theorem
6.48
it follows that

|\mathit{BS}^{**}_{L,U,D,n}(u)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)/D|\leq\sqrt{n}

with high probability. The same error analysis as for the first inequality yields the RHS expression, and by setting
D
as above the result follows.

∎

Remark 6.51

(Adapting
6.40
to the unbounded DP setting)
.

In the unbounded DP setting (i.e., when the size of the dataset is not known to the data analyst), the addition of
M\cdot n
as post-processing in step 3 will not be possible since
n
is potentially private. To adapt
6.40
to this setting, the user should use integer summation where overflow is handled with wraparound (i.e., modular summation – see
Section

6.2
) and set
M=0
(resulting in no subtraction from elements).

Additionally, the user should choose an upper bound
n_{\max}
on their best guess at the dataset size and set the discretization parameter
D=(U-M)\cdot n_{\max}/(2^{m})
. Then, the accuracy guarantees (e.g.,
Theorem

6.47
and
Theorem

6.48
) and overflow guarantees (e.g.,
Theorem

6.49
) will hold as long as
n\leq n_{\max}
; these guarantees break down for
n>n_{\max}
.

Alternatively, the user can use truncation (
6.24
) and the associated sensitivity,

\max\{\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n_{\max},\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}}\}.

Then, for
D=(U-M)\cdot n_{\max}/(2^{m})
, the accuracy and overflow guarantees will hold since the truncated dataset is guaranteed to have size
\leq n_{\max}
. (Note that the user should still set
M=0
since the length of the dataset is not known, so adding
M\cdot n_{\max}
could provide a signficant overestimate on the true result.)

Appendix A
Missing Proofs from Section
2

Lemma A.1

(Relating Metrics, Lemma
2.10
)
.

These metrics are related as follows.

1.

For
u,v\in\text{Vec}(\mathcal{D})
,

d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v)=\min_{\pi\in S_{\mathrm{len}(u)}}d_{\mathit{ID}}(\pi(u),v)\leq d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,v).

2.

For
u,v\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
,

d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v)=\min_{\pi\in S_{n}}d_{\mathit{Ham}}(\pi(u),v)\leq d_{\mathit{Ham}}(u,v).

3.

For
u,v\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
,

d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v)=2\cdot d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v).

4.

For
u,v\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
,

d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,v)\leq 2\cdot d_{\mathit{Ham}}(u,v).

Proof.

We prove each part below.

1.

Let
u,v\in\text{Vec}(\mathcal{D})
.

We first show that there exists a permutation
\pi\in S_{\mathrm{len}(u)}
such that
d_{\mathit{ID}}(\pi(u),v)\leq d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v)
.

We begin by constructing
v^{\prime}
such that
d_{\mathit{ID}}(v,v^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v)
and
h_{v^{\prime}}=h_{u}
. We show that we can use
d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v)
insertions and deletions on
v
to get
v^{\prime}
such that
h_{v^{\prime}}=h_{u}
. We create
v^{\prime}
from
v
as follows: (1) for all
z\in\mathcal{D}
such that
h_{v}(z)>h_{u}(z)
, we delete
h_{v}(z)-h_{u}(z)
copies of
z
from
v
; (2) for all
z\in\mathcal{D}
such that
h_{v}(z)<h_{u}(z)
, we insert
h_{v}(z)-h_{u}(z)
copies of
z
to
v
. By
Definition

2.7
, then,

d_{\mathit{ID}}(v^{\prime},v)\leq d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v).

(9)

The resulting vector
v^{\prime}
also has the property that, for all
z\in\mathcal{D}
,
h_{v^{\prime}}(z)=h_{u}(z)
, so
h_{u}=h_{v^{\prime}}
. By
Lemma

2.4
,
h_{u}=h_{v^{\prime}}
implies that there exists a permutation
\pi\in S_{\mathrm{len}(u)}
such that
\pi(u)=v^{\prime}
. Substituting this into
Eq.

9
, we get

d_{\mathit{ID}}(\pi(u),v)\leq d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v).

(10)

We next show that, for all permutations
\pi\in S_{\mathrm{len}(u)}
, we have
d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v)\leq d_{\mathit{ID}}(\pi(u),v)
, which means that
d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v)\leq\min_{\pi\in S_{\mathrm{len}(u)}}d_{\mathit{ID}}(\pi(u),v)
. This is true because, for all permutations
\pi\in S_{\mathrm{len}(u)}
,
d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v)=d_{\mathit{Sym}}(\pi(u),v)\leq d_{\mathit{ID}}(\pi(u),v)
, with the equality following from the fact that
h_{\pi(u)}=h_{u}
.

2.

Let
u,v\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
.

We first show that there is a permutation
\pi\in S_{n}
such that
d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v)=d_{\mathit{Ham}}(\pi(u),v)
.

We begin by constructing
v^{\prime}
such that
d_{\mathit{Ham}}(v,v^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v)
and
h_{v^{\prime}}=h_{u}
. We create
v^{\prime}
from
v
as follows: (1) for all
z\in\mathcal{D}
such that
h_{v}(z)>h_{u}(z)
, replace
h_{v}(z)-h_{u}(z)
copies of
z
with some value
\diamond\not\in\mathcal{D}
; (2) for all
z\in\mathcal{D}
such that
h_{v}(z)<h_{u}(z)
, replace
h_{u}(z)-h_{v}(z)

\diamond
values with
z
. By
Definition

2.8
, then,

d_{\mathit{Ham}}(v^{\prime},v)\leq d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v).

(11)

The resulting vector
v^{\prime}
also has the property that, for all
z\in\mathcal{D}
,
h_{v^{\prime}}(z)=h_{u}(z)
, so
h_{u}=h_{v^{\prime}}
. By
Lemma

2.4
,
h_{u}=h_{v^{\prime}}
implies that there exists a permutation
\pi\in S_{n}
such that
\pi(u)=v^{\prime}
. Substituting this into
Eq.

11
, we get

d_{\mathit{Ham}}(\pi(u),v)\leq d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v).

(12)

We next show that, for all permutations
\pi\in S_{n}
, we have
d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v)\leq d_{\mathit{Ham}}(\pi(u),v)
, which means that
d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v)\leq\min_{\pi\in S_{n}}d_{\mathit{Ham}}(\pi(u),v)
. This is true because, for every permutation
\pi\in S_{n}
,

d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v)=d_{\mathit{CO}}(\pi(u),v)\leq d_{\mathit{Ham}}(\pi(u),v),

with the equality following from the fact that
h_{\pi(u)}=h_{u}
.

3.

Let
u,v\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
. Then,

\displaystyle d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{D}}|h_{u}(z)-h_{v}(z)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathcal{D}\text{ s.t.}\\
h_{u}(z)>h_{v}(z)\end{subarray}}\left(h_{u}(z)-h_{v}(z)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathcal{D}\text{ s.t.}\\
h_{v}(z)>h_{u}(z)\end{subarray}}\left(h_{v}(z)-h_{u}(z)\right)

\displaystyle=d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v)+d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v)

\displaystyle=2\cdot d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v).

4.

Let
u,v\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
, and let
d_{\mathit{Ham}}(u,v)=c
. Let
\mathcal{I}
be the set of all indices
i^{*}
such that
u_{i^{*}}\neq v_{i^{*}}
. We note that, to make it so that
u_{i^{*}}=v_{i^{*}}
, we can perform 1 insertion and 1 deletion to change
u_{i^{*}}
to
v_{i^{*}}
. We note that the cardinality of
\mathcal{I}
is
c
. Therefore, a total of (at most)
2c
insertions and deletions need to be performed to change
u
into
u^{\prime}
such that
u^{\prime}=v
. By the definition of
d_{\mathit{ID}}
, then,
d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,v)\leq 2c=2\cdot d_{\mathit{Ham}}(u,v)
.

∎

Lemma A.2

(Lemma
2.14
)
.

The dataset metrics
d_{\mathit{Sym}},d_{\mathit{CO}},d_{\mathit{Ham}}
, and
d_{\mathit{ID}}
all satisfy the path property.

Proof.

By Definitions
2.5
,
2.6
,
2.7
,
2.8
and the fact that the image of the histogram function is
\mathbb{N}
it is clear that all of
d_{\mathit{Sym}},d_{\mathit{CO}},d_{\mathit{Ham}}
, and
d_{\mathit{ID}}
satisfy Condition 1 of the Path Property.

For Condition 2, we show constructively how to build the sequence
u^{0},u^{1},\ldots,u^{d}
for each of the metrics. Let
u,v
be datasets such that
d(u,v)=d
for some
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.

1.

For
d_{\mathit{Sym}}
, let set
S
be the symmetric difference of
u
and
v
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{u}
be the set of indices
k
such that
u_{k}\in S
, and similarly for
\mathcal{J}_{v}
. Wlog, assume that
|\mathcal{I}_{u}|\leq|\mathcal{J}_{v}|
(otherwise, swap
u
and
v
in what follows). To construct the
u^{(i)}
, we iteratively apply the following two steps. To go from
u^{(i)}
to
u^{(i+2)}
, first pick one of the indices
k\in\mathcal{I}_{u}
and delete it from
u^{(i)}
, which yields vector
u^{(i+1)}
. Next, insert into
u^{(i+1)}
element
v_{j}
such that
j\in\mathcal{J}_{v}
, never repeating either index
k
or
j
in the process. After
2|\mathcal{I}_{u}|
steps, to continue constructing the
u^{(i)}
iteratively we insert elements
v_{j}
for all the remaining
j\in\mathcal{J}_{v}
one at a time (without repeating any index
j
). Hence, after
|\mathcal{J}_{v}|-|\mathcal{I}_{u}|
more steps,
u^{(i)}=v
.

2.

For
d_{\mathit{CO}}
, to construct each
u^{(i)}
we iteratively change an element of
u
which is different from
v
(when viewed as multisets). It follows directly from the definition of
d_{\mathit{CO}}
that this procedure requires
d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v)=d
steps.

3.

For
d_{\mathit{Ham}}
, we apply the same procedure, except that
u
and
v
are now viewed as ordered vectors.

4.

For
d_{\mathit{ID}}
, we apply the same procedure as in the case of
d_{\mathit{CO}}
, where we do one insert or one delete operation at a time. It then follows directly from the definition of
d_{\mathit{ID}}
that this procedure requires
d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,v)=d
steps.

∎

Lemma A.3

(Convert Sensitivities, Lemma
2.15
)
.

We can convert between sensitivities in the following ways.

1.

For every function
f:\text{Vec}(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}f\leq\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}f
.

2.

For every function
f:\mathcal{D}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}f\leq\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}f
.

3.

For every function
f:\mathcal{D}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}f\leq 2\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}f
.

4.

For every function
f:\mathcal{D}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}f\leq 2\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}f
.

Proof.

We use Lemma
2.10
to prove each part. Let each
f
below be defined as described in its respective part above.

1.

By Lemma
2.10
, we see that, for all
u,v\in\text{Vec}(\mathcal{D})
, we have
d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,v)\leq d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,v)
. Therefore, for all
u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}
, we have
u\simeq_{\mathit{Sym}}u^{\prime}
. By the definition of sensitivity in Definition
2.11
, this means that
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}f=\max_{u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}}|f(u)-f(u^{\prime})|\leq\max_{u\simeq_{\mathit{Sym}}u^{\prime}}|f(u)-f(u^{\prime})|=\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}f.

2.

By Lemma
2.10
, we see that, for all
u,v\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
, we have
d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,v)\leq d_{\mathit{Ham}}(u,v)
. Therefore, for all
u\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}
, we have
u\simeq_{\mathit{CO}}u^{\prime}
. By the definition of sensitivity in Definition
2.11
, this means that
\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}f=\max_{u\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}}|f(u)-f(u^{\prime})|\leq\max_{u\simeq_{\mathit{CO}}u^{\prime}}|f(u)-f(u^{\prime})|=\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}f.

3.

We have:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}f

\displaystyle=\max_{u\simeq_{\mathit{CO}}u^{\prime}}|f(u)-f(u^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{u\simeq_{\mathit{CO}}u^{\prime}}\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}f\cdot d_{\mathit{Sym}}(u,u^{\prime})\quad\text{(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thrm:apply-path-prop})}

\displaystyle=\max_{u\simeq_{\mathit{CO}}u^{\prime}}\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}f\cdot 2\cdot d_{\mathit{CO}}(u,u^{\prime})\quad\text{(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:metric-relate})}

\displaystyle=2\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}f,

so
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}f\leq 2\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}f.

4.

We have:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}f

\displaystyle=\max_{u\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}}|f(u)-f(u^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{u\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}}\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}f\cdot d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,u^{\prime})\quad\text{(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thrm:apply-path-prop})}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{u\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}}\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}f\cdot 2\cdot d_{\mathit{Ham}}(u,u^{\prime})\quad\text{(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:metric-relate})}

\displaystyle=2\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}f,

so
\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}f\leq 2\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}f.

∎

Appendix B
Missing Proofs from Section
4

Theorem B.1

(Idealized sensitivities of
\mathit{BS}_{L,U}
and
\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}
, Theorem
4.4
)
.

The sensitivities of the bounded sum function are the following.

1.

(Unknown
n
.)
\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}=\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}=\max\{|L|,U\}.

2.

(Known
n
.)
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}=\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}=U-L.

Proof.

Each part is proven below.

1.

Let
u,u^{\prime}\in\text{Vec}(\mathbb{R}_{[L,U]})
be two datasets such that
u\simeq_{\mathit{Sym}}u^{\prime}
. From the formal definition of a histogram in Definition
2.2
, we observe that, for all
v\in\text{Vec}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathit{BS}_{L,U}(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathrm{len}(v)}v_{i}=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{R}}h_{v}(z)\cdot z
, where the last sum is well defined because
h_{v}(z)\neq 0
for only finitely many values of
z
. Because
u\simeq_{\mathit{Sym}}u^{\prime}
, we know that there is at most one value
z^{*}
such that
|h_{u}(z^{*})-h_{u^{\prime}}(z^{*})|=1
, and that, for all
z\neq z^{*}
, we have
|h_{u}(z)-h_{u^{\prime}}(z)|=0
.

We can then write the following expressions.

\displaystyle\left|\mathit{BS}_{L,U}(u)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U}(u^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{z\in\mathbb{R}}h_{u}(z)\cdot z-\sum_{z\in\mathbb{R}}h_{u^{\prime}}(z)\cdot z\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{z\in\mathbb{R}\setminus z^{*}}h_{u}(z)\cdot z-\sum_{z\in\mathbb{R}\setminus z^{*}}h_{u^{\prime}}(z)\cdot z\right|

\displaystyle+\left|h_{u}(z^{*})\cdot z^{*}-h_{u^{\prime}}(z^{*})\cdot z^{*}\right|

\displaystyle=0+\left|z^{*}\cdot(h_{u}(z^{*})-h_{u^{\prime}}(z^{*}))\right|

\displaystyle\leq|z^{*}|

\displaystyle\leq\max\{|L|,U\},

with the final inequality following from the fact that all values are clamped to the interval
[L,U]
, so the largest difference in sums arises when
z^{*}=\max\{|L|,U\}
.

By the definition of sensitivity, then,
\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}\leq\max\{|L|,U\}
. By Theorem
2.15
, we also have
\Delta_{ID}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}\leq\max\{|L|,U\}
.

For the lower bound, consider the datasets
u=[0],u^{\prime}=[0,\max\{|L|,U\}]
. We note that
u\simeq_{\mathit{ID}}u^{\prime}
. We also note that
|\mathit{BS}_{L,U}(u)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U}(u^{\prime})|=\max\{|L|,U\}
. This means, then, that
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}\geq\max\{|L|,U\}
. By the contrapositive of Theorem
2.15
, then,
\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}\geq\max\{|L|,U\}
.

Combining these upper and lower bounds on the idealized sensitivity tells us, then, that

\Delta_{\mathit{Sym}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}=\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}=\max\{|L|,U\}.

2.

Let
u,u^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two datasets such that
u\simeq_{\mathit{CO}}u^{\prime}
. By Lemma
2.10
, there is a permutation
\pi\in S_{n}
such that
\pi(u)\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}
. This means there is at most one index
i^{*}
such that
\pi(u)_{i^{*}}\neq u^{\prime}_{i^{*}}
. We can then write the following expressions.

\displaystyle|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(u)-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(u^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(\pi(u))-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(u^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\pi(u)_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}(u^{\prime}_{i})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(\pi(u)_{i^{*}})-(u^{\prime}_{i^{*}})\right|

\displaystyle\leq U-L.

with the final inequality following from the fact that all values are clamped to the interval
[L,U]
, so the largest difference in sums arises when, without loss of generality,
\pi(u)_{i^{*}}=U
and
u^{\prime}_{i^{*}}=L
.

By the definition of sensitivity, then,
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}\leq U-L
. By Theorem
2.15
, we have
\Delta_{Ham}\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}\leq U-L
.

For the lower bound, consider the datasets
u=[L]
and
u^{\prime}=[U]
. Then,
u\simeq_{\mathit{Ham}}u^{\prime}
and
|\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)-\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u^{\prime})|=U-L
. By the contrapositive of Theorem
2.15
, it follows that
\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}\geq U-L
.

Combining these upper and lower bounds on the idealized sensitivity, we then conclude that

\Delta_{\mathit{Ham}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}=\Delta_{\mathit{CO}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}=U-L.

∎

Appendix C
Missing Proofs from Section
5

Theorem C.1

(Theorem
5.17
)
.

Let
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}:\text{Vec}(T_{[L,U]})\to T
be iterative bounded sum on the type
T
of
(k,\ell)
-bit floats. Let
j,m\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
0<j\leq k
and
-(2^{\ell-1}-2)\leq m\leq 2^{\ell-1}-2-j-k
.
Then for
L=0
,
U=2^{k+m}
, and
n=j\cdot 2^{k}+2
, there are datasets
u,v\in\text{Vec}(T)
where
len(u)=n
and
\mathrm{len}(v)=n-1
such that
d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,v)=1
and

|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(v)|\geq 2^{k+j+m}.

In particular,

\frac{\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}}{\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}}\geq 2^{j}.

Proof.

Let
f(x)
be defined as
x\mapsto(2^{x}+2^{x-k})\cdot 2^{m}
, and consider the datasets

\displaystyle u={}

\displaystyle[2^{k+m},2^{k+m},f(0)_{1},\ldots,f(0)_{2^{k}},

\displaystyle f(1)_{2^{k}+1},\ldots,f(1)_{2\cdot 2^{k}},\ldots,

\displaystyle f(j-1)_{(j-1)\cdot 2^{k}+1},\ldots,f(j-1)_{j\cdot 2^{k}}],

and
v
such that
v_{i}=u_{i+1}
for all
i
(so the first term of
u
is not in
v
). Note that
d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,v)=1
. Additionally, we consider
m=0
until the conclusion of the proof.

We first evaluate
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)
. We begin by seeing that the sum of the first two terms can be computed exactly:
2^{k}\oplus 2^{k}=2^{k+1}
.

We next use
Lemma

2.27
to show that the sum of the first three terms cannot be computed exactly:
2^{k+1}+f(0)=2^{k+1}+1+2^{-k}\in[2^{k+1},2^{k+2})
, so
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(2^{k+1}+f(0))=2
. However,
2^{k+1}+f(0)
is not a multiple of 2, so by
Lemma

2.27
,
2^{k+1}+f(0)
cannot be represented exactly as a
(k,\ell)
-bit float. This means that we must use banker’s rounding (described in
Definition

2.33
) to determine the value of
2^{k+1}\oplus f(0)
. The value
2^{k+1}+f(0)
is between the adjacent
(k,\ell)
-bit floats
2^{k+1}+0
and
2^{k+1}+2
. It is closer to
2^{k+1}+2
, so we have
2^{k+1}\oplus f(0)=2^{k+1}+2
.

Having determined the result of adding
f(0)_{1}
to the intermediate sum
2^{k+1}
, we now determine the result of adding the remaining
f(0)
values. By reasoning similar to the logic used above, we find that for every addition of
f(0)_{i}
, adding
f(0)=1+2^{-k}
has the effect of adding
2
. Therefore,
2^{k+1}\oplus f(0)_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus f(0)_{2^{k}}=2^{k+2}
.

We use
Lemma

2.27
to show that none of the remaining additions are exact, and we show the rules of banker’s rounding affect the computation of
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)
. By
Lemma

2.27
, because
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(2^{k+2})=4
and
2^{k+2}+f(1)
is not a multiple of 4, we must use banker’s rounding (described in
Definition

2.33
) to determine the value of
2^{k+2}\oplus f(1)
. Reasoning similar to the logic above shows that adding each of the
f(1)=2+2^{-k+1}
terms has the effect of adding
4
, so
2^{k+2}\oplus f(1)_{k+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus f(1)_{2\cdot 2^{k}}=2^{k+3}
. Such logic follows for all of the floating point additions, resulting in an overall sum of
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)=2^{k+j+1}
.

We now evaluate
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(v)
. The general structure of the proof is similar, but the resulting sums are different due to different effects of banker’s rounding.

We next use
Lemma

2.27
to show that the sum of the first three terms cannot be computed exactly:
2^{k}+f(0)=2^{k}+1+2^{-k}\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})
, so the
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(2^{k}+f(0))=2^{0}
. However,
2^{k+1}+f(0)
is not a multiple of 1,
so by
Lemma

2.27
,
2^{k}+f(0)
cannot be represented exactly as a
(k,\ell)
-bit float. This means that we must use banker’s rounding (described in
Definition

2.33
) to determine the value of
2^{k}\oplus f(0)
. The value
2^{k}+f(0)
is between the adjacent
(k,\ell)
-bit floats
2^{k}+0
and
2^{k}+1
. It is closer to
2^{k}+1
, so we have
2^{k}\oplus f(0)=2^{k}+1
.

Having determined the result of adding
f(0)_{1}
to the intermediate sum
2^{k}
, we now determine the result of adding the remaining
f(0)
values. By reasoning similar to the logic used above, we find that for every addition of
f(0)_{i}
, adding
f(0)=1+2^{-k}
has the effect of adding
1
(note in the calculation of
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)
above that adding
f(0)=1+2^{-k}
has the effect of adding
2
– this is a critical difference). Therefore,
2^{k}\oplus f(0)_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus f(0)_{2^{k}}=2^{k+1}
.

We now use
Lemma

2.27
to show that none of the remaining additions are exact, and we show the rules of banker’s rounding affect the computation of
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(v)
. By
Lemma

2.27
, because
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(2^{k+1})=2
and
2^{k+1}+f(1)
is not a multiple of 2, we must use banker’s rounding (described in
Definition

2.33
) to determine the value of
2^{k+1}\oplus f(1)
. Reasoning similar to the logic above shows that adding each of the
f(1)=2+2^{-k+1}
terms has the effect of adding
2
, so
2^{k+1}\oplus f(1)_{k+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus f(1)_{2\cdot 2^{k}}=2^{k+2}
. Such logic follows for all of the floating point additions, resulting in an overall sum of
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)=2^{k+j}
.

We see that
|\mathit{BS}^{*}(u)-\mathit{BS}^{*}(v)|=2^{k+j}
.

From the bounds
-(2^{\ell-1}-2)\leq m\leq 2^{\ell-1}-2-j-k
, we see that
m
only affects the exponent of floating point values in the proof and does not affect whether values are representable as
(k,\ell)
-bit normal floats or the direction of rounding. All values in the proof above, then, can be multiplied by
2^{m}
, so, for all
m
such that
-(2^{\ell-1}-2)\leq m\leq 2^{\ell-1}-2-j-k
,
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}\geq 2^{k+j+m}
.

We observe that
U=2^{k+m}
and
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}=\max\{|L|,U\}=U
. Therefore,

\frac{\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}}{\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}}\geq 2^{j}.

∎

Theorem C.2

(Theorem
5.19
)
.

Let
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}:\text{Vec}(T_{[L,U]})\to T
be iterative bounded sum on the type
T
of
(k,\ell)
-bit floats. Additionally, let
a,j\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
2\leq j<k
,
-(2^{\ell-1}-2)\leq a
,
-(2^{\ell-1}-2)+1+k\leq j+a\leq 2^{\ell-1}-1
(these conditions ensure that values are representable as
(k,\ell)
-bit floats),
n=2^{j}
,
m=n/2
. Then, for

U=2^{a},L=-\left(\frac{U\cdot m}{2^{k}}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2^{k}}\right),

there are datasets
u,v\in\text{Vec}(T_{[L,U]})
with
d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,v)=1
such that

|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(v)|\geq\frac{n^{2}}{2^{k+3}}\cdot U+U.

In particular,

\frac{\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}}{\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}}=\frac{n^{2}}{2^{k+3}}+1.

Proof.

Let

x=\left(\frac{U\cdot m}{2^{k}}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2^{k}}\right),

and consider the datasets

u=[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m-1},U_{m},x_{1},L_{2},x_{3},L_{4},\ldots,x_{m-1},L_{m}]

and

v=[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m-1},x_{1},L_{2},x_{3},L_{4},\ldots,x_{m-1},L_{m}].

where, for all
i
,
L_{i}=L
and
U_{i}=U
.
Note that
u
and
v
are equivalent with the exception that
v
contains
m-1
copies of
U
rather than
m
copies of
U
, so
d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,v)=1
.

We first show that
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m-1}]=(m-1)\cdot U
and
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}]=m\cdot U
. We take the approach of showing that all intermediate sums computed in the calculation of
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}]
can be represented exactly as
(k,\ell)
-bit floats, which is done by first showing that all of these intermediate sums are multiples of their
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}
and then applying
Lemma

2.27
.

We observe that
\{U,\ldots,m\cdot U\}
is the set of intermediate sums that result from calculating
\mathit{BS}_{L,U}[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}]
. Let
\mathcal{U}
denote this set
\{U,\ldots,m\cdot U\}
. We note that
m\cdot U=2^{a+j-1}
, so
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)=2^{a+j-1-k}
. For all
x\in\mathcal{U}
, then,

\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(x)\leq\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)=2^{a+j-1-k}.

Note that
j<k
, so
2^{a+j-1-k}
necessarily divides
U=2^{a}
. All such
x
are multiples of
U=2^{a}
, so they are necessarily multiples of
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(x)
. By
Lemma

2.27
, all
x\in\mathcal{U}
can be represented exactly as
(k,\ell)
-bit floats, so by
Lemma

2.38
,
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m-1}]=(m-1)\cdot U
and
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}]=m\cdot U
.

We now evaluate
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(u)
. We use
Lemma

2.27
to show that
\mathit{BS}_{L,U,n}(u)=m\cdot U+x
is not exactly representable as a
(k,\ell)
-bit float, and we show how to calculate the result
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(u)
.

As shown above, the sum of the first
m
terms is

\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}]=m\cdot U.

Thus,
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m},x]=m\cdot U\oplus x
. We note that

m\cdot U+x\in[m\cdot U,m\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)].

Because
m\cdot U+x
is closer to
m\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)
,
m\cdot U\oplus x=m\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)
. We now consider the addition of
L
,
m\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)\oplus L
. We see that

m\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)+L\in[m\cdot U,m\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)],

and
m\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)+L
is closer to
m\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)
, so
m\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)\oplus L=m\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)\oplus L
. Similar logic applies for all
x,L
, in which adding
L
has the effect of adding
0
and adding
x
has the effect of adding
\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}(m\cdot U)
for a total sum of
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)=m\cdot U+\frac{m^{2}\cdot U}{2^{k+1}}
.

We now evaluate
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U,n}(v)
. As shown above, the sum of the first
m-1
terms corresponds to
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m-1}]=(m-1)\cdot U
. Thus,
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}[U_{1},\ldots,U_{m-1},x]=(m-1)\cdot U\oplus x
. We note that

(m-1)\cdot U+x\in[(m-1)\cdot U,(m-1)\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}((m-1)\cdot U)].

Because
(m-1)\cdot U+x
is closer to
(m-1)\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}((m-1)\cdot U)
,
(m-1)\cdot U\oplus x=(m-1)\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}((m-1)\cdot U)
. We now consider the addition of
L
,
(m-1)\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}((m-1)\cdot U)\oplus L
.
We see that

\displaystyle(m-1)\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}((m-1)\cdot U)+L

\displaystyle\in[(m-1)\cdot U,(m-1)\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}((m-1)\cdot U)],

and
(m-1)\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}((m-1)\cdot U)+L
is closer to
(m-1)\cdot U
, so
(m-1)\cdot U+\mathit{ULP}_{(k,\ell)}((m-1)\cdot U)\oplus L=(m-1)\cdot U
. Similar logic applies for all
x,L
, in which adding
x
and
L
in succession has the effect of adding
0
for a total sum of
\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(v)=(m-1)\cdot U
.

Therefore,

|\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(u)-\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}(v)|=\frac{m^{2}\cdot U}{2^{k+1}}+U=\frac{n^{2}\cdot U}{2^{k+3}}+U,

so, because
d_{\mathit{ID}}(u,v)=1
,
d_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}=\frac{m^{2}\cdot U}{2^{k+1}}+U
. We observe that
\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}=\max\{|L|,U\}=U
, so

\frac{\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}^{*}_{L,U}}{\Delta_{\mathit{ID}}\mathit{BS}_{L,U}}=\frac{n^{2}}{2^{k+3}}+1.

∎
